and defined through
[{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Phi,{\Gamma})]=[\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}\circ{\mathcal{U}}^{{\Gamma}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}^{-1}]
, where
\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}^{-1}
is a homotopy inverse
66
66

66

Here, we use the fact that a
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-morphism is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if it is a homotopy equivalence [Section 3.7 of
[
73
]
for a statement as well as references therein]. This implies that there exists a (non-canonical)
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}^{-1}:{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\rm poly}}}\to{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
such that
\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}^{-1}\sim\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\rm poly}}}}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}\sim\mathrm{id}_{{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}}}
. Contrarily to its counterpart
\mathcal{U}^{\Gamma}
, the family of
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-automorphisms
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{D}}
does depend on the existence of a Drinfel’d associator (although the explicit choice does not matter due to the equivariance relation
{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Phi\cdot{\Gamma}^{\prime},{\Gamma})={\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Phi,\text{Ad}_{{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\Gamma})
with
\text{Ad}_{{\Gamma}^{\prime}}{\Gamma}={\Gamma}^{\prime}\cdot{\Gamma}\cdot{\Gamma}^{\prime}{}^{-1}
).
of the representative
\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}
.

The regularity of the action of
\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
on
{\rm\mathsf{SQI}}
can be restated as follows: for any pair of homotopy classes of stable formality morphisms
[\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}]
and
[\mathcal{U}_{\Phi^{\prime}}]
, there exists a unique element
\Gamma\in\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
such that
[\mathcal{U}_{\Phi^{\prime}}]=[\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}\circ\mathcal{U}^{\Gamma}]
, for any representatives
\mathcal{U}_{\Phi}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\Gamma}
. The space
{\rm\mathsf{SQI}}
of homotopy classes of stable formality morphisms can then be fully explored by composition with
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-automorphisms of
{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
induced from
\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
. Such a reasoning can also be shown to hold at the level of quantization maps. Indeed, each arrow appearing in Diagram
5.2
is a (homotopy class of)
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphisms and thus induces a bijection between (equivalence classes of) Maurer–Cartan sets [
cf.
footnote
2
] as:

\textstyle{\quad\mathsf{FPoiss}\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{{\Gamma}}]}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi}]}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi\cdot{\Gamma}}]}
\textstyle{\quad\mathsf{Star}\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Phi,{\Gamma})]}
\textstyle{\quad\mathsf{FPoiss}\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi}]}
\textstyle{\quad\mathsf{Star}\quad}

\textstyle{\quad[\pi]\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{{\Gamma}}]}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi}]}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi\cdot{\Gamma}}]}
\textstyle{\quad[*]\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\Phi,{\Gamma})]}
\textstyle{\quad[\pi^{\prime}]\quad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi}]}
\textstyle{\quad[*^{\prime}]\quad}

Mimicking the above reasoning allows to span the whole space of stable quantization maps
{\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi}}
by composition with stable deformation maps
{\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{{\Gamma}}}
induced from
\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
[
cf.
Proposition
4.13
]. The resulting characterisation of the action of
\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
on stable quantization maps in terms of stable deformations has the merit to make certain features relatively explicit. As an example, it follows from the previous reasoning that formal Poisson structures
[\pi]
which are insensitive to stable deformations
67
67

67

That is, such that
{\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{{\Gamma}}}([\pi])=[\pi]
for all
{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
.
admit canonical quantizations
i.e.
their quantum class is unique
68
68

68

In other words, the associated (class of) star products
[*]=\hat{\mathcal{U}}_{\Phi}([\pi])
does not depend on the choice of Drinfel’d associator
\Phi
.
. Straightforward reasoning on the number of derivatives involved in stable deformations [see
e.g.
eq.(
5.1
)] entails that Poisson bivectors whose local description is at most quadratic in coordinates admit a unique local quantization. This is in particular the case for constant Poisson bivectors (and in particular for symplectic manifolds in Darboux coordinates) which are uniquely (locally) quantized by the Groenewold–Moyal star product
[
56
,
100
]
,
cf.
eq.(
4.13
). Slightly less trivial is the Kostant–Souriau–Kirillov Poisson bracket – defined on the dual of any Lie algebra – which is linear in coordinates. The latter admits two known quantizations, namely the Gutt
[
60
,
45
]
and Kontsevich
[
85
]
star products. According to the previous reasoning, these two star products must belong to the same equivalence class. However, they do not coincide, as shown for example in
[
85
,
72
,
113
,
34
,
9
]
. Rather, they are related via an isomorphism given by the Duflo map (
cf. e.g.
Theorem 14 in
[
47
]
).

5.2
Courant algebroids (
n=2
)

{section:Courant algebroids}

The present section applies the results of Section
4
to symplectic Lie
2
-algebroids. The latter notion identifies with the one of Courant algebroids that we now review, following the presentation
à la
Dorfman (
cf. e.g.
[
71
]
for details and
[
87
]
for a historical account).

Definition 5.1

(Courant algebroid)
.

{defCourantD}

A Courant algebroid is a quadruplet
(E,{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}},\mathcal{D},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
where:

•

The pair
(E,{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}})
is a
pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle

i.e.

–

E\to\mathscr{M}
is a vector bundle over the smooth manifold
\mathscr{M}
. We will denote
({\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M}),\cdot)
the commutative associative algebra of functions on
\mathscr{M}
and
*:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})\otimes\Gamma\left(E\right)\to\Gamma\left(E\right)
the module structure on fibers of
E
. The latter satisfies the associativity relation
f*(g*X)=(f\cdot g)*X
for all
f,g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})
and
X\in\Gamma\left(E\right)
.

–

The map
{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}}:\Gamma\left(E\right)\otimes\Gamma\left(E\right)\to{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})
satisfies the following conditions:

1.

\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathscr{M}\right)
-bilinear
i.e.
\left\langle f*X,Y\right\rangle_{E}=\left\langle X,f*Y\right\rangle_{E}=f\cdot\left\langle X,Y\right\rangle_{E}

2.

symmetric
i.e.
\left\langle X,Y\right\rangle_{E}=\left\langle Y,X\right\rangle_{E}

3.

non-degenerate
i.e.
\left\langle X,Y\right\rangle_{E}=0
for all
Y\in\Gamma\left(E\right)

\Leftrightarrow

X=0
.

A bilinear form satisfying these conditions will be referred to as a
fiber-wise metric
.

•

The pair
(\mathcal{D},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
is a
Courant–Dorfman structure
on
(E,{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}})

i.e.

–

\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E}:\Gamma\left(E\right)\otimes\Gamma\left(E\right)\to\Gamma\left(E\right)
is a
{\mathbb{K}}
-bilinear form on the fibers of
E
called the
Dorfman bracket
.

–

\mathcal{D}:{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})\to\Gamma\left(E\right)
is a
{\mathbb{K}}
-linear derivation
i.e.
\mathcal{D}(f\cdot g)=f*\mathcal{D}f+g*\mathcal{D}f
for all
f,g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})
. The derivation
\mathcal{D}
defines a
{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})
-linear map
\rho:\Gamma\left(E\right)\to\Gamma\left(T\mathscr{M}\right)
called the
anchor
as
\rho_{X}[f]=\left\langle X,\mathcal{D}f\right\rangle_{E}
for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})
,
X\in\Gamma\left(E\right)
.

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

The Dorfman bracket satisfies the Jacobi identity in its Leibniz form:

\displaystyle\left[X,\left[Y,Z\right]_{E}\right]_{E}=\left[\left[X,Y\right]_{E},Z\right]_{E}+\left[Y,\left[X,Z\right]\right]_{E}\text{ for all }X,Y,Z\in\Gamma\left(E\right)

so that the pair
(\Gamma\left(E\right),\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
is a
{\mathbb{K}}
-Leibniz algebra.

2.

The symmetric part of the Dorfman bracket is controlled by the derivation
\mathcal{D}
as:

\displaystyle\left[X,Y\right]_{E}+\left[Y,X\right]_{E}=\mathcal{D}\left\langle X,Y\right\rangle_{E}\text{ for all }X,Y\in\Gamma\left(E\right)\,.

3.

The fiber-wise metric
{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}}
is compatible with the Courant–Dorfman structure
(\mathcal{D},

\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\left\langle X,\mathcal{D}\left\langle Y,Z\right\rangle_{E}\right\rangle_{E}=\left\langle\left[X,Y\right]_{E},Z\right\rangle_{E}+\left\langle Y,\left[X,Z\right]_{E}\right\rangle_{E}=0\text{ for all }X,Y,Z\in\Gamma\left(E\right)\,.

Introducing a basis
\left\{e_{a}\right\}_{a=1,\ldots,\dim E}
of the space of sections
\Gamma\left(E\right)
allows to provide a component expression of the Courant algebroid maps as follows:

•

In components, the fiber wise metric reads
\left\langle X,Y\right\rangle_{E}=\kappa_{ab}\,X^{a}\,Y^{b}
where the constant matrix
\kappa
satisfies:

1.

\kappa
is symmetric
i.e.
\kappa_{ab}=\kappa_{ba}
.

2.

\kappa
admits an inverse
\kappa^{-1}
such that
\kappa^{ac}\kappa_{cb}=\delta^{a}_{b}
with
\delta
the Kronecker delta.

•

The component expression for the Courant–Dorfman structure
(\mathcal{D},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
is captured by a pair
(\rho_{a}{}^{\mu},T_{abc})
, where
T_{abc}
is totally skewsymmetric. Explicitly, we have:

–

\mathcal{D}
-map:
\mathcal{D}f=\kappa^{ab}\rho_{b}{}^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}f\,e_{a}

–

Anchor:
\rho_{X}[f]=X^{a}\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f

–

Dorfman bracket:
\left[X,Y\right]_{E}=\big(\rho_{X}[Y^{a}]-\rho_{Y}[X^{a}]-T_{bc}{}^{a}X^{b}Y^{c}+\kappa^{ab}\rho_{b}{}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}X^{c}\kappa_{cd}Y^{d}\big)e_{a}

where indices are raised and lowered with
\kappa
.

It can be checked that the defining conditions of a Courant algebroid are satisfied if and only if the pair
(\rho_{a}{}^{\mu},T_{abc})
satisfies the set of conditions:

1.

{\mathcal{C}_{1}}^{\mu\nu}:=\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}\kappa^{ab}\rho_{b}{}^{\nu}=0

2.

{\mathcal{C}_{2}}^{\mu}_{ab}:=\rho_{c}{}^{\mu}\kappa^{cd}T_{dab}+2\,\rho_{[a}{}^{\lambda}\,\partial_{\lambda}\rho_{b]}{}^{\mu}=0

3.

{\mathcal{C}_{3}}_{abcd}:=\frac{1}{4}T_{e[ab}\kappa^{ef}T_{cd]f}+\frac{1}{3}\rho_{[a}{}^{\mu}\,\partial_{\mu}T_{bcd]}=0
.

Comparing this set of constraints with (
1
)-(
3
) allows to relate Courant algebroids with symplectic Lie
2
-algebroids (or
{\rm\mathsf{NPQ}}
-manifolds of degree 2). The precise nature of this relation is articulated in the following theorem:

Theorem 5.2

(D. Roytenberg
[
105
]
)
.

{thmRoytenberg}

•

{\rm\mathsf{NP}}
-manifolds of degree 2 are in bijective correspondence with pseudo-Euclidean vector bundles.

•

{\rm\mathsf{NPQ}}
-manifolds of degree 2 are in bijective correspondence with Courant algebroids.

The Poisson algebra of functions associated with a given
{\rm\mathsf{NP}}
-manifold
\mathcal{V}
of degree 2
(
or equivalently the
2
-Schouten algebra
{\mathcal{T}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}=\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)[2]
)
was referred to as the
Rothstein algebra
in
[
74
]
. The latter can be interpreted as the deformation complex of Hamiltonian functions on
\mathcal{V}
.
Via the second point of Theorem
5.2
, this can be rephrased as saying that the Rothstein algebra controls the deformation theory of Courant–Dorfman structures
(\mathcal{D},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
on the pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle
(E,{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}})
– where
E
is defined by the fibration (
2.2
) as
\mathscr{M}\leftarrow E[1]\leftarrow\mathcal{V}
– according to the following sequence of bijective correspondences:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})\Leftrightarrow(\rho_{a}{}^{\mu},T_{abc})\Leftrightarrow{\mathscr{H}}=\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}\,\xi^{a}p_{\mu}+\frac{1}{6}\,T_{abc}\,\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\xi^{c}

(5.12)

where the right-hand side makes use of the local set of coordinates
\Big\{\underset{0}{x^{\mu}},\underset{1}{\xi^{a}},\underset{2}{p_{\mu}}\Big\}
[Example
2.2
].

The supergeometric interpretation of Courant algebroids provided by Theorem
5.2
will allow us to apply the results of Section
4.3
in order to generate new stable deformation formulas for Courant–Dorfman structures
(\mathcal{D},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
on a given pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle
(E,{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}})
.
69
69

69

In this sense, the procedure does not deform the
full
Courant algebroid structure since the bilinear form
{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}}
remains undeformed.
As noted in Lemma
4.8
, the zeroth cohomology of the connected part of the full Kontsevich graph complex in
d=3
is one dimensional and spanned by the triangle class
i.e.
H^{0}(\mathsf{fGC}^{\text{\rm con}}_{3})={\mathbb{K}}\left\langle L_{3}\right\rangle
,
cf.
Figure
4
. This result ensures
(
cf.
Proposition
4.9
)
that there exists a
unique
stable deformation of Courant algebroids that we now explicitly characterise.

Letting
(E,{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}})
be a pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle, we use the bijective correspondence (
5.12
) in order to associate to each Courant–Dorfman structure
(\mathcal{D},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{E})
on
(E,{\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{E}})
the corresponding Hamiltonian function
{\mathscr{H}}=\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}\,\xi^{a}p_{\mu}+\frac{1}{6}\,T_{abc}\,\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\xi^{c}
with associated homological vector field
{\mathsf{Q}}:=\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{\cdot}\big\}_{\omega}
. Via Corollary
4.16
, the stable Hamiltonian flow associated with
L_{3}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\dot{\mathscr{H}}={\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{(3)}_{3}\big(L_{3}\big)({\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes 3})=\mu_{3}\big(\Delta_{12}\,\Delta_{23}\,\Delta_{31}\,({\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes 3})\big)

where the expression of the operator
{\rm\mathsf{Rep}}_{3}^{(3)}
follows (
4.8
) with
\Delta
given by [
cf.
eq.(
4.10
)]:

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}_{(i)}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\mu}^{(j)}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{\mu}^{(i)}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}_{(j)}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{a}_{(i)}}\,\kappa^{ab}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{b}_{(j)}}.

Explicitly, the triangle Hamiltonian flow maps any Hamiltonian function
{\mathscr{H}}
towards the associated Rothstein cocycle
\dot{\mathscr{H}}\in H^{3}(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)|{\mathsf{Q}})
defined as
\dot{\mathscr{H}}=\dot{\rho}_{a}{}^{\mu}\,\xi^{a}p_{\mu}+\frac{1}{6}\,\dot{T}_{abc}\,\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\xi^{c}
where:

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{a}{}^{\mu}

\displaystyle={\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{(3)}_{3}\big(L_{3}\big)({\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes 3})_{a}{}^{\mu}\quad=\quad\raisebox{-12.91663pt}{\hbox{\hbox to47.07pt{\vbox to38.88pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.71669pt\lower-31.06956pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Here, the directed arrows stand for space-time derivatives while undirected arrows represent contractions of fiber indices with the non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form
\kappa
. The local expression of the Hamiltonian flow induced by the triangle cocycle can be equivalently expressed in components as:

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{a}{}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\rho_{b}{}^{\lambda}\,\partial_{\lambda}\rho_{a}{}^{\nu}\,\partial_{\nu}\rho^{b|\mu}+\rho_{b}{}^{\lambda}\,\partial_{\lambda}\rho_{c}{}^{\mu}\,T_{a}{}^{bc}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\dot{T}_{abc}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}{\rho_{a}{}^{\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho_{b}{}^{\lambda}}\,\partial_{\lambda}{\rho_{c}{}^{\mu}}-\partial_{\mu}{\rho_{a}{}^{\lambda}}\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho_{b}{}^{\mu}}\,\partial_{\lambda}{\rho_{c}{}^{\nu}}-T_{a}{}^{de}T_{bdf}T_{ce}{}^{f}

\displaystyle\quad+3\,\rho_{d}{}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}{\rho_{[a}{}^{\nu}}\partial_{\nu}{T_{bc]}{}^{d}}+3\,\rho_{d}{}^{\mu}T_{[a}{}^{de}\partial_{\mu}{T_{bc]e}}

where indices are raised and lowered with
\kappa
. Consistently with Proposition
4.14
, it can be checked that
\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{\dot{\mathscr{H}}}\big\}_{\omega}=0
modulo the relations (
1
)-(
3
) coming from
\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{{\mathscr{H}}}\big\}_{\omega}=0
.

It should be emphasised that the situations corresponding to
d=2
and
d=3
are drastically different. In the case
d=2
, the zeroth cohomology is the “dominant” degree
i.e.
contains an infinite number of non-trivial classes leading to infinitely many stable deformations of Poisson manifolds,
cf.
Section
5.1
. On the contrary, for
d=3
, the zeroth cohomology is one-dimensional and thus yields a unique stable deformation of Courant–Dorfman structures given by (
5.13
). The “dominant” degree of
\mathsf{fGC}_{3}
being
-3

(
cf.
Section
3.4
)
, it would be desirable to find a construction mapping elements of
H^{-3}(\mathsf{fGC}_{3})
to Courant–Dorfman deformations. This is achieved through Weyl factors, as we now show.

Weyl factors for Courant algebroids.

Recall from Section
3.4
that
H^{-3}(\mathsf{fGC}_{3})
is spanned by trivalent graphs modulo IHX relations [see Figure
6
and eq.(
3.9
)]. Proposition
4.18
ensures that each element
\gamma\in H^{-3}(\mathsf{fGC}_{3})
is mapped to a stable Weyl factor for Courant algebroids. Explicitly, given a trivalent graph
\gamma
, Hamiltonian functions
{\mathscr{H}}
can be mapped to Weyl factors
\Omega_{\gamma}({\mathscr{H}})\in H^{0}(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\left(\mathcal{V}\right)|{\mathsf{Q}})\simeq\text{\rm Ker }\mathcal{D}
. The explicit local expression of the Weyl factor
\Omega_{\gamma}({\mathscr{H}})
associated to a given graph
\gamma\in H^{-3}(\mathsf{fGC}_{3})
with
N
vertices is given by
\Omega_{\gamma}({\mathscr{H}}):={\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{(3)}_{N}({\gamma})({\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes N})
. We now exemplify this construction by displaying the Weyl factors associated to the simplest trivalent graphs. The simplest example of trivalent graph is given by the “
\Theta
” graph
being the only connected trivalent graph with
N=2
vertices. The latter yields the following Weyl factor:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Theta}({\mathscr{H}}):={\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{(3)}_{2}(\Theta)({\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes 2})

(5.14)

\displaystyle=T_{abc}\,T^{abc}+6\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho_{a}{}^{\lambda}}\,\partial_{\lambda}{\rho}^{a|\nu}

and the equality
\mathcal{D}\,\Omega_{\Theta}=0
follows from
\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{{\mathscr{H}}}\big\}_{\omega}=0
. In particular, this ensures that
\dot{\mathscr{H}}_{\Theta}:=\Omega_{\Theta}({\mathscr{H}})\cdot{\mathscr{H}}
is a Rothstein cocycle.
70
70

70

It can be checked by brute-force computation that the vector space of universal Hamiltonian flows for
N=3
is of dimension 2 and spanned by the triangle flow (
5.13
) and the Weyl
\Theta
-flow defined from (
5.14
).

The next to simplest case is given by the graphs
A
and
B
from Figure
6
for
N=4
yielding:

\displaystyle\Omega_{A}({\mathscr{H}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{(3)}_{4}\big(A\big)({\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes 4})=T_{abc}\,T^{abd}\,T^{cef}\,T_{def}+4\,\partial_{\mu}{\rho_{a}{}^{\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho_{b}{}^{\mu}}\,\partial_{\lambda}{\rho^{a|\rho}}\,\partial_{\rho}{\rho^{b|\lambda}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-8\,\partial_{\mu}{\rho_{a}{}^{\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho^{a|\lambda}}\,\partial_{\lambda}{\rho_{b}{}^{\rho}}\,\partial_{\rho}{\rho^{b|\mu}}+4\,\partial_{\mu}{\rho^{a|\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho_{d}{}^{\mu}}T_{abc}\,T^{dbc}

\displaystyle\Omega_{B}({\mathscr{H}})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{(3)}_{4}\big(B\big)({\mathscr{H}}^{\otimes 4})

\displaystyle\hskip-18.49411pt=T_{abc}\,T^{a}{}_{de}\,T^{bdf}\,T^{ce}{}_{f}-8\,\partial_{\mu}{\rho_{a}{}^{\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho_{b}{}^{\lambda}}\,\partial_{\lambda}{\rho_{c}{}^{\mu}}\,T^{abc}-6\,\partial_{\mu}{\rho_{a}{}^{\nu}}\,\partial_{\nu}{\rho_{b}{}^{\lambda}}\,\partial_{\lambda}{\rho^{a|\rho}}\,\partial_{\rho}{\rho^{b|\mu}}.

Together with
\Omega_{\Theta}({\mathscr{H}})^{2}
(corresponding to the disconnected graph
\gamma=\Theta\cup\Theta
), these are the only Weyl factors available for
N=4
. Note however that the trivalent graphs
A
and
B
can be related through the IHX relation (
3.9
) as
A\sim 2\,B
,
cf.
footnote
40
. This ensures that their respective Weyl factors are related via
\Omega_{A}({\mathscr{H}})=2\,\Omega_{B}({\mathscr{H}})
where the correspondence can be shown by making use of the constraints (
1
)-(
3
) coming from
\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{{\mathscr{H}}}\big\}_{\omega}=0
.

Relation to quantization.

Remarkably, Kontsevich’s original quantization formula (
i.e.
with standard propagator) can be interpreted
[
85
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
as a 3-point function in the path integral quantization of a 2-dimensional topological field theory
– the
Poisson
\sigma
-model
, introduced in
[
65
,
68
,
108
]
– whose source is of dimension
d=2
and whose target is the (shifted) cotangent bundle associated to the Poisson manifold. The graphs appearing in Kontsevich’s stable formula can therefore be interpreted from the point of view of quantum field theory as Feynman diagrams associated with the quantification of the Poisson
\sigma
-model.

As mentioned previously, the Poisson
\sigma
-model constitutes the first rung of an infinite ladder of AKSZ
\sigma
-models
[
6
]
associating to any symplectic Lie
n
-algebroid a topological field theory of dimension
d=n+1
. An interesting open problem concerns the possibility of generalising such interplay between deformation quantization results (on the algebraic side) and quantization of AKSZ-type of models (on the field theoretic side) to higher values of
n
.

For
n=2
, the relevant AKSZ
\sigma
-model was constructed by D. Roytenberg in
[
106
]
(
cf.
[
67
]
for an earlier derivation from consistent deformations of a Chern–Simons gauge theory coupled with a
0
-dimensional
BF
theory). Such model associates to any Courant algebroid a canonical 3-dimensional topological field theory – the
Courant
\sigma
-model
. From the field theory side, quantization of the Courant
\sigma
-model within the Batalin–Vilkovisky formalism
[
12
]
has been considered in
[
64
,
63
]
(
cf.
also
[
66
]
for a discussion of observables in general AKSZ
\sigma
-models).

On the algebraic side, a possible candidate for the quantum notion associated with Courant algebroids is given by
vertex algebroids
, as introduced in
[
54
]
from truncation of vertex algebras
[
55
]
. Indeed, it was shown in
[
17
]
that the semi-classicalisation of (commutative) vertex algebroids yields a Courant algebroid. This suggests a formulation of a deformation quantization problem for Courant algebroids, similar to the one formulated in
[
14
,
13
]
for Poisson manifolds.

Although it is outside of the scope of the present paper to address the quantization problem for Courant algebroids, we note that some insights can be gained
71
71

71

We refer to
[
99
]
for more details on the partition of deformation quantization problems according to graph cohomology.
from the classification of graph cocycles in
H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{fGC}_{3})
:

1.

H^{1}(\mathsf{fGC}_{3})=\mathbf{0}
:
The existence of stable formality morphisms for Courant algebroids is unobstructed.

2.

H^{0}(\mathsf{fGC}_{3})={\mathbb{K}}
:
The space of stable formality morphisms for Courant algebroids is of dimension
1
.

The first statement asserts that the
2
-Schouten algebra
({\mathcal{T}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathsf{S}})
is rigid – at least in the “stable setting” –
i.e.
it does not admit non-trivial deformations as a
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-algebra. In other words, the graded Lie algebra
{\mathcal{T}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
is intrinsically formal
72
72

72

A graded Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},\left[\cdot,\cdot\right])
is said to be
intrinsically formal
if any
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-algebra
(\mathfrak{g},l)
restricting to the dg Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{h},0,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right])
(
i.e.
with vanishing differential) in cohomology
(
i.e.
H(\mathfrak{g},l)=(\mathfrak{h},0,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right])
)
is formal.
in the stable setting. The homotopy transfer theorem thus ensures that, given a dg Lie algebra (or
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-algebra)
{\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
such that
H^{\bullet}({\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}})
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{T}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
as a graded Lie algebra and a quasi-isomorphism of complexes
\mathcal{U}_{1}:{\mathcal{T}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
given by stable formulæ, the “HKR-type” map
\mathcal{U}_{1}
can always be prolongated to a full stable
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
quasi-isomorphism
\mathcal{U}:{\mathcal{T}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{D}^{(2)}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
, which in turn would provide a quantization map for Courant algebroids.

Recall that such reasoning
73
73

73

We refer to Section 4.1 of
[
99
]
for more details.
constituted the initial rationale behind the introduction of the graph complex
\mathsf{fGC}_{2}
in
[
84
]
. However, in this case, it is a hard open conjecture that
H^{1}(\mathsf{fGC}_{2})=\mathbf{0}
so that M. Kontsevich had to rely on different methods in order to prove his formality theorem for Poisson manifolds. On the contrary, for Courant algebroids, it is straightforward to show the rigidity of the
2
-Schouten algebra (see above) so that one can use the original Kontsevich approach to prove a formality theorem for Courant algebroids.

From the second statement, we learn that such morphism is not unique, but rather that formality morphisms form a
1
-dimensional space. Consequently, there should exist a one-parameter family of stable quantization maps for Courant algebroids
74
74

74

As in the Poisson case, some Courant algebroids are insensitive to deformations so that their associated quantum class is unique
i.e.
independent of the parameter. This can be shown to be the case of
exact
Courant algebroids [see
e.g.
[
71
]
for a definition] which are insensitive to the triangular deformation defined by (
5.13
).
. This is again in sharp contrast with the Poisson case for which
H^{0}(\mathsf{fGC}_{2})
is infinite-dimensional (being isomorphic to the Grothendieck–Teichmüller Lie algebra
\mathfrak{grt}_{1}
) and thus the space of formality morphisms (and consequently also the one of stable quantization maps for Poisson manifolds) forms an infinite-dimensional space (in bijective correspondence with the space of Drinfel’d associators).

However, as shown in Corollary
4.19
, there is a way to consistently deform Courant algebroids via trivalent vertices (modulo IHX relations) using Weyl deformation maps. Composing such deformations with a given quantization map yields an infinite-dimensional space of quantizations for Courant algebroids. In other words, despite the fact that the space of stable formality morphisms is finite-dimensional (of dimension 1), the space of universal quantizations of a given Courant algebroid is infinite-dimensional, the arbitrariness being encoded into trivalent graphs (on top of the triangle graph).
